This was the weakest relocation on size (2,285 → 1,484 bytes, an 0.8 KB return on a whole new file) because §4's bullets were already compressed; taken for consistency with (ii), not for the bytes. **(iv) §5.1's two contents enumerations → `docs/reference/data-architecture-detail.md` §5.1**, with a representative handful kept inline — deliberately conservative, as §5.1 is the second-most-cited section (109 citations) and the lists are how a new field is classified quickly. **Considered and rejected:** replacing §6.2's abridged layer table with prose saved 73 bytes and cost scannability. **Nothing broke:** only `scripts/check-section-refs.ts` reads CLAUDE.md, and only its top-level headings; every §N and subsection number is retained as a stub, so the 663 inbound citations still resolve. **The 4.6 KB net against ~7.4 KB moved is recorded in the Rev 43 narrative**: a stub carrying an invariant costs 30–60% of the bullets it replaces, and the Document Map grows a row per subsidiary file (5,114 → 5,610 bytes). Narrative: `docs/reference/claude-md-revision-history.md` Rev 43.

- **2026-09-13 — Every Apple-only `%@` placeholder leaves canonical, and `check-locale-strings.ts` stops the next one being written (closes `OI-I18N-01`, raises `OI-I18N-02`; GitHub Issue #342).** CLAUDE.md §17.2 has made `{0}`/`{1}` the canonical placeholder form since 2026-09-03, which `sync-locales.ts` renders as `%1$@` for Apple and `%1$s` for Android. **Ten keys predated the rule and still held a literal `%@`** — `CONSUMABLE_ORDER_PACK_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_A11Y_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_SCORE_A11Y_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_VALUE_FORMAT`, `SESSION_RMSSD_VALUE_FORMAT`, `SETUP_ERROR_IMPEDANCE_FAILED`, `SETUP_ERROR_ZONE_MISSING`, `SETUP_IMPEDANCE_FAIL_A11Y`, `SETUP_IMPEDANCE_PASS_A11Y`, `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_NO_TYPE`. **Why it had never failed:** the generators convert placeholders by matching `{n}`, so a value carrying `%@` matches nothing and both copy it through verbatim — which is exactly what Apple's `String(format:)` wants. The only platform reading these keys was the one the defect is invisible on. On Android `Resources.getString(id, args)` runs `java.util.Formatter`, where `@` is an unknown conversion: `UnknownFormatConversionException`, **a crash rather than a mis-render**. Four of the ten are accessibility strings and two are setup *error* strings — the paths least likely to be hit in manual testing and worst to crash on. This is the second half of the defect found while building the Android consent dashboard (2026-09-08, `OI-CONSENT-01`), which converted the ten consent-surface keys in the same state and left these rather than widening that change. **Twelve keys were converted, not ten.** `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_WITH_TYPE` (`%1$@, %2$@`) and `ZONE_SOCKET_POSITION_LABEL` (`Socket %1$d — %2$@`) carry the **positional** spelling of the same Apple-only conversion and throw the identical exception on the identical code path; a gate that rejects `%@` and accepts `%1$@` would have closed the defect and left the hole, which is the opposite of what §17 is for. **A partial conversion inside one value is worse than none**, and this decided the shape of the fix: `sync-locales.ts` escapes a literal `%` to `%%` only once the value contains a `{n}`, so leaving `%1$d` beside a newly-added `{1}` would have emitted `%%1$d` and rendered the specifier itself to the user. Every conversion in a touched value therefore converts together — `ZONE_SOCKET_POSITION_LABEL` became `Socket {0} — {1}` whole. **One iOS call site changed, and only one.** `ZoneModuleInfo.label(for:)` passed `Int(socketID)` to what had been `%1$d` and is now `%1$@`, so the number is converted with `String(socketID)` at the call site, which is §17.2's stated rule for a numeric argument on Apple. The other eleven call sites were audited and already pass `String` — `NPNumberFormatter.decimal0/1`, `packDisplayPrice`, the joined electrode/socket label lists, and `channelNames` — so they are untouched: positional and non-positional `%@` consume the same arguments in the same order, and `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_WITH_TYPE`'s generated Apple value is byte-identical to what it was before. **The gate is the substance of the item, not the ten fixes.** `checkInterpolationSyntax` in `scripts/check-locale-strings.ts` now rejects any Apple-only `@` conversion — `%@` and `%n$@` — in a canonical value, alongside the existing `\(…)` and `${…}` rules. The self-test previously drove only `scanSource`, so the three interpolation rules had never been seen to reject; it now carries ten canonical-value cases (34 total), including both `@` spellings as failures and `%d`, a literal `{0}%`, and a `{n}` value as passes — so the rule is proven to reject as well as to pass, which is this file's own standard for calling something a gate. Verified end-to-end by reintroducing `%@` into `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_NO_TYPE` and watching the gate exit 1. **Android resolution was verified at runtime, not only at build.** The generated `values/strings.xml` was driven through `java.util.Formatter` — the same code path `Resources.getString(id, args)` takes — and all twelve keys resolve with no leftover specifier (`Socket 12 — Frontal Left`, `C3: signal good`, `Coherence score 7.4`); the two pre-fix values throw `UnknownFormatConversionException` on that same path, which is the crash `OI-I18N-01` predicted and had never been able to demonstrate. **A third platform is fixed in passing:** web's `t()` substitutes `{n}` and nothing else, so these twelve keys would have rendered `%@` literally there too. **Not verified by compiler:** this environment has no Swift toolchain and no Android SDK, so neither app is built here — `ios-ci.yml` and `android-ci.yml` are what compile them, and both already run on a `locales/**` change (2026-09-08). **Deliberately not widened, and raised instead as `OI-I18N-02`:** fourteen keys still carry `%d`, `%.1f` or `%1$d`. Those are valid conversions in *both* `String(format:)` and `java.util.Formatter`, so they render on both platforms and crash on neither — a different defect, costing §17.2's real purpose (a canonical `{0}` is positional by construction, so a translation may reorder its arguments; a bare `%d` is consumed in source order). Converting them is **not** mechanical the way this was, because `{0}` generates `%1$@` on Apple and every numeric argument would need `String(…)` at its call site — it needs a call-site audit, which is why it is a separate item rather than folded in here.

- **"Never asked" and "said stop" become different states, so §6.0's *withdrawal stops ALL research data flows* survives the categories a withdrawal leaves ticked (2026-09-13 — CLAUDE.md Rev 45):** Raised as an explicit non-decision in the `OI-CONSENT-03` entry above (*"distinguishing 'never asked' from 'said stop' needs a field `ResearchConsentState` does not carry and is a policy decision, not a code tidy"*) and settled here on request. **The gap was real and, once Rev 44's ingestion gate existed, live.** `blanketConsentGranted == false` is true of a user who never turned L3 on and of one who turned it off; §6.0 treats them differently, because only the second stopped everything. Withdrawal **deliberately does not clear the nine L2 categories** — destroying a stored preference is its own decision, and `withdrawBlanketResearchConsent` has never done it — so a withdrawn user's stale checkboxes went on satisfying `hasAnyResearchConsent` and `ConsentEngine.admit()` went on admitting studies to them as consent requests. That is research data flowing after the user stopped it, which is the one thing §6.0's sentence forbids. **The fix is one field and one gate step.** `ResearchConsentState.blanketConsentWithdrawnAt` (Kotlin: `blanketConsentWithdrawnOnDay`) records the true→false transition; `blanketConsentWithdrawn` is `withdrawnAt != nil && !granted`; `admit()` refuses with the new `researchConsentWithdrawn` **before** the `hasAnyResearchConsent` check, because a withdrawn user usually still passes that test. **Three properties of the marker are load-bearing, and each is the kind a later refactor can drop without appearing to change anything.** (i) **The store sets it; no screen can.** The consent UI commits a whole `ResearchConsentState` and cannot see a transition, so `updateResearchConsent` carries the stored marker forward and overwrites it only on the transition — otherwise a screen holding a snapshot read before the withdrawal could commit it back and erase the record of the user having said stop. There is a test for exactly that stale commit. (ii) **Guarded on the transition, not the value** — the §6.2.5 shape, for the §6.2.5 reason: withdrawing blanket consent that was never granted stops nothing, and marking it would silently bar an L2 participant from studies they still consent to. (iii) **Re-granting clears the condition, not the record**, so a user who changes their mind resumes on the engagement-notification path while the withdrawal stays in the history. **The dashboards now render three research postures rather than two** on both platforms, because telling a withdrawn user *"You are asked about each study"* — the previous copy for everyone with L3 off — while the gate refuses every study they are sent is simply false; the new row names the date they stopped and where to start again. 2 canonical locale keys across all 11 files. **8 new unit tests per platform** (iOS 45→53, Android 40→48), including the stale-commit case, the never-granted case, and the re-grant. **`:core` compiled and its 220-test suite run green here**; `:app` and the iOS target are CI's first compile as before. **Not done, and not an oversight: no telemetry was added.** The request that prompted this also asked whether metrics on it would be useful, and they are not collectable. §6.0 tears down research analytics *on* blanket withdrawal precisely because it "signals the user does not want any data collection beyond basic device function", so an event reporting the withdrawal is the first thing the withdrawal forbids; and by §5.1's defining test a consent choice is a fact about the **person**, hence UHDR, which NeurOne never accesses (§5). Routing it through SHDR would additionally conflate the two consent subjects of §6.0, the warranty owner being possibly a clinic rather than the patient. Even per-descriptor *refusal* counts that look innocuous leak by complement — the server knows what it sent, so "sent, not refused-for-integrity, no extract" is a one-bit consent oracle, the §5.1 rule-2 shape. What the distinction does buy is local: the gate is correct, the refusal reasons are legible in a test, and **the user sees their own withdrawal on their own dashboard**. **One migration note, recorded rather than fixed:** a `ResearchConsentState` persisted before this field existed decodes with `nil`, so a user who withdrew earlier reads as "never asked" until they withdraw again. No device has shipped (pre-tooling design phase), so there is no such record; if that changes before launch the backfill is a one-line default, not a schema change. **No locked decision is changed.**
- **2026-09-13 — the accessory and companion-software roadmap gets an ordering, and more importantly a criterion for placing the next one (`NP-ACC-PRIORITY-001` Rev 1, Issue #344 — closes the general TODO raised 2026-08-30 in `docs/status/pending-decisions.md` §13.2; raises `OI-ACC-01…03`):** `NP-THERM-COOL-001` §8 **D-3** had ranked exactly two accessories against each other — TEC base-station chiller above hip ice pack, on value delivered — and in doing so exposed that `docs/reference/accessories-roadmap.md` carried **no relative ordering at all** and CLAUDE.md §2.3's consumables were priced but not sequenced. **Delivered: 18 items in 17 ranks, and the criterion that produced them.** The ranked list is the smaller half. A list alone is a snapshot — the next accessory raised has nowhere to go, and putting it somewhere re-opens every pair above and below it — so §3 states the criterion first and §5 applies it, and §3.5 makes placing a new item a lookup that touches **one class, not the list**. **The criterion is four tests applied lexicographically: capability class → reach → irreversibility of omission → engineering cost to ship.** Class is C1 access (the therapy does not reach the user at all without it — including the second form, a channel the platform delivers but a given user cannot receive), C2 binding relief (raises the term that *currently* binds `min(electrical, thermal, dose)` — D-3's own test stated generally), C3 continuity, C4 envelope (same therapy, wider conditions of use), C5 experience. **Three things about the criterion are decisions rather than presentation.** **(i) Lexicographic, not weighted — and this is the whole of D-3's holding.** D-3 did not trade the ice pack's real merits against the chiller's benefit: ~1–2 W against 56–188 W, preserved Mode 3 autonomy the chiller cannot have, and no mains dependency at all. It asked what each one *changes*, found one raises the binding term and the other does not, and stopped. **Any additive rubric weighting autonomy or draw meaningfully inverts a decision already taken**, so §4 falsifies the criterion against that pair per `NP-CONV-001` §8 and reproduces both its direction and its magnitude — D-3 said "low", and the ice pack lands **15th of 18** with only the two lens options and the Watch app beneath it, derived rather than fitted. §4 also records what is *not* falsified: no prior decision ranks *across* classes, so C1-over-C2-over-C3 is argued rather than tested, and that is the part of §3 most open to challenge. **(ii) No cost figure is an input at any test.** CLAUDE.md §2.1: every configuration cost figure is a **floor** excluding the uncosted term **U** (`OI-HEXTILE-02` has selected no 660/808 nm emitter, so `OI-HUB-C08` cannot close), and `OI-COST-10` forbids setting any price before `OI-HEXTILE-06`. A ranking consuming those numbers would assert a comparison the document set cannot make **and would silently re-rank itself when term U lands**; BOM deltas appear in §5 as reader context, explicitly labelled as not inputs. **(iii) Dependencies are recorded in a Gate column, never ranked** — each marked EXTERNAL (outside the project's control) or INTERNAL. Issue #344 asked for this, and §3.3 gives the operative reason: a gate's lifting date is not evidence about value, and a ranking that consumed gate dates would re-rank itself every time a trial slipped or a supplier moved — the re-litigation the document exists to prevent, arriving through the back door. The reading is *rank says what to build; the Gate column says what you can build now*. **C3 had to be split to survive contact with the safety architecture:** ordering nine consumables on reach alone contradicted what the interlocks do, so C3a (a **named** mechanism refuses the session — authentication for the intranasal sleeve, the >5× baseline impedance cutoff in `NP-FMEA-GEOM-001` FMEA-G02-01, the contact-confirmation hold in CLAUDE.md §4.2) sits above C3b (degrades, no refusal). "It would probably be bad" is C3b. **One placement is set by a regulatory declaration rather than judgement:** `NP-APP-ROADMAP-001` §6 declares every Apple Watch function session monitoring / UI aid and **not** therapeutic delivery, which forecloses C1–C4 — so the Watch app is C5 (rank 18) on the product's own filing position, despite the widest reach in its class and $0 hardware BOM. **One output is independent of the ranking entirely (§7):** the mastoid pad's temporal-wing anchor boss is free at first tooling cut and expensive as a retrofit, while its gate (#30, HOPE Phase 3, n=670) is EXTERNAL and will not lift on that schedule — so **provision it whichever way HOPE reads out**. The asymmetry is total and holds whether the pad ranks 1st, 10th or is excluded, which is why it is recorded rather than inferred from rank 1. It changes no tooling specification; it argues for a provision already described. **Three open items were found by building the list, each a question the document set could not answer:** `OI-ACC-01` — **nothing anywhere states whether the goggle assembly can be worn over corrective eyewear**, which swings the S3 Rx system (#25) between rank 2 (C1 access) and below rank 18 (C5 convenience), the largest single placement swing in §5, and is tooling-*relevant* because it is goggle-arm and lens geometry; `OI-ACC-02` — the audio cup foam's prompt is recorded as a **calendar** reminder in `docs/reference/commercial-model.md` §2.3 while CLAUDE.md §2.3 states every consumable prompt is measurement-triggered and never calendar-triggered, left unresolved deliberately because inventing a compression proxy in a documentation pass is the design work the invariant exists to force; `OI-ACC-03` — the cervical tcVNS gel pad is specified as a consumable in `docs/reference/modality-stack.md` and appears in no consumables table, so it has no price, interval, GM% or trigger. **Status DRAFT:** §9 asks for sign-off on **§3, not §5** — §5 is re-derivable by anyone holding §3, and signing off only §5 leaves the next accessory to re-open the list, which is the state #344 was raised to end. **CLAUDE.md was not touched** — the ranking is explicitly not an invariant and is expected to move, the Document Map's existing accessories row already routes there, and that file's first lines now route onward. No price set, no locked decision changed, no requirement altered.
- **The clinician-grant form's two unasked questions get asked, and the channel that would deliver a third gets specified rather than built (2026-09-13 — closes OI-CONSENT-04, OI-CONSENT-05, OI-CONSENT-06; issue #338):** Three items on one surface, all the same shape — §6 is locked and specifies the behaviour, and the app either does not implement it or implements it where nothing can reach it. **(1) `OI-CONSENT-04`: the picker decides the grant now.** `NewClinicianGrantView` rendered `ConsentEngine.useCaseLibrary` as a toggle list, held the answer in `selectedUseCases`, and built a `ClinicianConsentGrant` carrying only the tier — so §6.1's *clinicians select use cases, never data elements* was a question the user answered and nothing consumed. Resolution (1) of the two the item offered was taken: the grant carries `useCaseIDs` **and** an explicit initial `ClinicianAccessScope` whose elements `ConsentEngine.initialAccessScope()` derived from them. **Both, and not one, because they answer different questions**: the IDs are what the user consented to in their own terms, and the scope is what that came to on the day — **frozen, never re-derived**, so adding an element to a library entry later cannot reach backwards and widen every grant already made with nobody asked. The derived set is clamped to the tier, which remains the ceiling and the thing the subscription is priced at. **The library moved to keys, not English** (`CLINICIAN_USECASE_<ID>_NAME`/`_DESC`), which is what let it be ported to Android at all: `:core` is pure-JVM by design (ISC-2..4) and cannot reference `R.string`, so English in a shared table would be English no platform could translate — CLAUDE.md §17's module-level-tables-hold-keys rule, and the end of the `TODO(localization)` that had sat at the top of `ConsentEngine.swift`. The `:app` UI resolves each key through an explicit `when`, not an identifier lookup, so every key stays visible to `check-locale-strings.ts` and a missing one is a compile error rather than a blank label. The two orphaned engine functions resolved with it: `minimumNecessaryElements(for:)` is what the form derives from, and `consentDocument(...)` renders live under the picker — **minus `plainLanguageSummary`**, an English paragraph assembled from element `rawValue`s, which are the persisted Codable values rather than display text. The document carries no prose of its own now, the rule `ClinicianAccessDifferential` was already built under. Android's form gains the picker it deliberately did not port when `OI-CONSENT-01` landed, and its tier-derived can-see / cannot-see lists now follow the selection. **(2) `OI-CONSENT-06`: the retroactive question is asked on a first grant.** Resolution (1) — ask it — not (2), default to prospective-only, **and the fact that (2) was the more conservative option is what decided against it.** *When in doubt → UHDR* is the right default for a field nobody will be asked about and the wrong answer to a question the specification says to ask: §6.1 does not say retroactive access should be narrow, it says the two decisions are *always presented as separate decisions*, and a default — either default — presents neither. The expansion flow's two-step control is reused verbatim on both platforms: the history question comes afterwards, on its own, with both answers as buttons of equal weight and neither preselected. It costs a step on the most common consent flow in the app; that is the cost the item named. The `effectiveScopes` fallback stays and is now reached only by grants written before this, because *the tier's elements, from the grant date, prior data included* is what a bare tier has always meant and a grant made under it has to keep meaning it. **(3) `OI-CONSENT-05`: the channel is specified, and what shipped is a port rather than a transport.** `NP-SW-PORTAL-API-001` is the device-facing API the item said had to come first, and it confirms the item's own guess that `OI-CONSENT-03` and this one share one transport — not for convenience, but because each would otherwise re-derive three properties: pull-only delivery, admission by signature rather than by transport, and a response identical whether or not the user has consented to anything. Three of its findings are consequences of locked text rather than choices. **The headset is not a party** — CLAUDE.md §4.6 gives it no mode that talks to a server, so "device-facing" means app ↔ service, which is exactly what makes §5.3's on-device anonymisation implementable. **No push, and polling that does not vary with the user's state** — a push token is an identifier NeurOne would hold that is neither the device ID nor the warranty token (§5.1, §6.0), and an app that polls only when the user is contactable tells the server the user is contactable: §5.1's rule 2 applied to a network trace rather than a record, which is also why the app acknowledges *delivery* and never *admission* ("admitted descriptor X" and "refused descriptor X" are the same consent oracle by complement, because the service knows what it sent). **And the verifying key may not travel over the channel it authenticates**, which is why `OI-CONSENT-07` is a key-management item and not a coding one — a channel delivering both the object and its key proves only that one party sent both, which TLS already proves. In code: `ClinicianPortalChannel` with `RefusingClinicianPortalChannel` as the only shipped implementation, and `ClinicianPortalSync` ingesting in a load-bearing order — **identity, then the device's own records, then the differential** — so a forged request naming a grant this device never issued is refused *as forged*, never as unknown, and the refusal cannot be read backwards to learn which grants the device holds. `addExpansionRequest` moves from `pending` to a new **`ingest`** declaration in `check-consent-reachability.ts` that names the ingesting file and fails if it is missing or does not call the method; declaring it `ui` would have passed the gate on a caller that is not a UI at all. The same change makes **`pending` fail once a caller appears**, the rule waivers already carried and for the same reason — a declaration that a path does not exist has to break when it does, or the gate vouches for a lie. Five self-test cases added (14 total). **Two things are deliberately still missing and named rather than faked:** the clinician-identity anchor, and an answer for §6.1's *asks questions* — whose text is user-authored content about the user's own care, hence UHDR by §5.1's defining test, hence E2E to the clinician or it does not ship, never plaintext through a NeurOne service. **No endpoint or schema is specified on purpose**, because writing a URL and a JSON body before the key question is settled would make the unsolved part look solved — the same reason `RefusingStudyDescriptorVerifier` refuses instead of logging. **17 new unit tests per platform** (Android `ConsentStoreTests` 23 → 39, `:core` suite 199 → 215 green here; iOS `ConsentEngineTests` and `ConsentStoreTests` gain the parallel cases). 17 canonical locale keys across all 11 files, one removed (`CLINICIAN_GRANT_BUTTON`, superseded by the two-step flow's own buttons). **`:app` and the iOS target were not compiled here** — no Android SDK, no Xcode toolchain — so CI is their first compile, as it was for `OI-CONSENT-01`; every `R.string` reference was checked by hand against the generated `strings.xml` first, and the new Swift file is registered in the committed `project.pbxproj` because CI builds that rather than regenerating from `project.yml`. **No locked decision is changed, and no CLAUDE.md revision was needed**: §6.1's sentences were already true of the specification, and this is what makes them true of the code.
- **NP-FW-HUB-001 Rev 1 authored, closing OI-DOC-01 — and the specification's first act was to find a blocking capability gap (2026-09-13, Issue #339).** The hub control program had been cited as governed by NP-FW-HUB-001 since 2026-05-16 — registered in NP-DHF-001 §5.4 and `docs/status/document-register.md`, named at the head of `firmware/CMakeLists.txt` and `app/web/src/lib/hubCompiler.ts`, referenced from 48 files — while `docs/np_fw_hub_001.md` did not exist. **Resolution option (1) taken:** the specification is written against the shipped `firmware/hub_control/` behaviour and issued as a **new Rev 1 dated 2026-09-13**, not back-dated to the register's 2026-05-16, because that date recorded a document that was never written and a fabricated history is not a design record. Option (2) — retire the number and re-scope under NP-SW-001 — was rejected: the 48 citations resolve against the section anchors as written, so re-pointing them would have moved the cost rather than paid it. **Contents:** §2 program structure, four FreeRTOS tasks, transport framing and fault philosophy · §3 module registry · §4 the signed session-descriptor wire format, now at v3 · §5 session runner · §6 UHDR/SHDR session log and durability model · §7 safety-MCU SPI · §7a cervical-VNS re-enable manager · §8.1–§8.9 module drivers · §9 the IEC 62304 SW-02 Class B argument. **24 requirements met by the code, and 4 explicitly NOT met and carried as open items (§10.2)** — a specification written after the code that quietly matched the code everywhere would be describing, not specifying. 23 decisions, 10 risk rows, 12 design-review checks, 6 open items. **The blocking finding, `OI-FWHUB-01`: transcranial PBM — CLAUDE.md §3 modality ①, the product's primary optical modality — has no dispatchable path at all.** Two independently-documented facts compose into one nobody had stated. (a) `np_mod_pbm_*` is reachable only through slots 0–4 in `k_slot_probes`, and those are the retired zone-module slots that `np_protocol_verify_and_parse()` rejects (`slot_id < NP_HUB_SLOT_FIRST_VALID`). (b) The replacement addressing, `NP_PROTO_TARGET_SOCKET_MASK`, parses correctly and resolves through `np_module_map`, and is then **dropped** by `dispatch_command()`, which handles no target kind but `NP_PROTO_TARGET_SLOT` — tracked as OI-HUB-SOCKET-01. Each half is a deliberate fail-closed choice and correct on its own terms: falling back to the slot path would dispatch an eleven-socket frontal-left command to whatever sits in slot 0, and a wrong-site dose is not recoverable where a missed one is. **Their conjunction is that no transcranial PBM protocol can execute on the shipped firmware**, and nothing in the repository said so, because the document that would have said it did not exist. Intranasal PBM (slot 9) is unaffected. The root is structural: there are two module inventories — the slot-indexed `np_module_registry` and the socket-indexed `np_module_map` — and the socket lattice replaced the zone slots in the wire format, the parser and the map, but **not in dispatch**. **Two further findings.** Three source files (`np_mod_t2_stubs.c`, `np_adaptation_log.c`, `np_adaptation_log.h`) headed their banners *"NP-FW-HUB-001 Rev 2"* against a document that had no Rev 1 — re-pointed to Rev 1 §8.9 and §6.4 in the same change (OI-FWHUB-02, closed), and recorded rather than fixed silently because a revision citation invented against an absent document is OI-DOC-01 in miniature. And `NP_HUB_PROTO_VERSION` has reached 3 — v2 replaced the five-bit `slot_mask` with `slot_id` plus a variable target block because 80 sockets do not fit in five bits and are not slots; v3 grew `np_mod_tdcs_params_t` by OI-CHARGE-04's declared electrode area, bumped rather than absorbed because a v2 tDCS block is a *different length* for the same modality code and the un-bumped symptom would read as a corrupt descriptor rather than an out-of-date one — while the register still described Rev 1. **The general lesson, and the reason the absence was expensive rather than untidy: a register entry naming a document that cannot be read cannot go visibly stale.** §4 is now the wire format of record that `hubCompiler.ts` compiles against, and that no mechanical check enforces their agreement — contrary to NP-CONV-001 §8, which requires cross-artifact interface agreement to be verified by diff and never by review — is raised as OI-FWHUB-03. **Two live defects surfaced by writing §2 against the code, and both are the argument for having done this at all.** `OI-FWHUB-07`: `np_mod_reg_scan()` runs at bring-up step 5 with the SHDR zone-auth callback, which stamps each record with `s_device_session_count` — still 0, because the count is read at step 7 — and `np_log_init()` then sets `s_shdr_pos = 0U`, discarding the records the scan just wrote; boot-time module authentication therefore never reaches SHDR, for two independent reasons, and the source comment above `np_log_backend_init()` claims the files open *"before the session logger writes any record"* while the call three lines above it already contradicts that. Not a hazard — SHDR is device-condition fleet telemetry that nothing on the device reads back — and near-inert today because the zone slots those records describe are themselves retired, but it will bite the moment the callback follows the socket lattice. `OI-FWHUB-08`: the source banner and the document register both said "four tasks" since 2026-05-16 where the code creates **five**; `task_protocol_rx` is the fifth, separate from `task_hub_control` on purpose because `np_runner_run()` blocks for the whole session. **Both are fixed in the same change, and gated rather than merely fixed.** `np_log_backend_init()` and `np_log_init()` now precede `np_mod_reg_scan()`, which keeps `np_safety_spi_init()`'s `GAIN_SEL` precedence intact — the two constraints are independent and both are satisfiable at once — and the misleading comment is corrected. The banner and the register now say five. The reason a gate and not just a fix: `np_hub_control_app_main()` is **ARM-cross-only** — `firmware/hub_control/src/` sits in `HUB_SOURCES`, which compiles under the arm-none-eabi toolchain and in **no host test** — so no unit test can reach that sequence and nothing ever had, which is exactly how a defect dating to 2026-05-16 survived every merge. `scripts/check-hub-bringup-order.ts` parses the function, asserts four ordering constraints and counts the banner's declared task set against `xTaskCreate()`, refuses (exit 2) rather than passing vacuously if the entry point or any named call is absent, and strips comments and string literals so a call named only in prose cannot satisfy a constraint. Per `NP-CONV-001` §8 it was **falsified against the pre-fix commit itself**, not only against fixtures: run with `--root` over `git show HEAD:...np_hub_control_main.c` it reports exactly the two violations this change repairs. Wired into `tooling-ci.yml:hub-bringup-order` with its own relevance list (`firmware/hub_control/**`, the gate, the workflow), and `check-gate-coverage` plus `check-ci-scope` both pass with it registered. **Not fully discharged:** the document is unapproved (OI-FWHUB-06), so `21 CFR §820.30(d)`'s review-and-approve limb remains outstanding even though its documentation limb is now met; the register says so rather than implying a completed review.
- **NP-FW-NVRAM-001 Rev 2 — the Map 3 store is buildable, and EMMC-CFG-02's raw-write clause is refuted rather than outweighed (2026-09-13, Issue #339).** Rev 1 found that `EMMC-CFG-02` reserves *"a dedicated Config partition journal area (raw-write, no LittleFS overhead for high-frequency updates)"* while `EMMC-FS-01` gives Config's LittleFS instance `block_size` 4,096 × `block_count` 4,096 = 16 MiB, **the whole partition** — so the journal area has no address space — and deliberately stopped at the finding (OI-NVRAM-01, BLOCKING). A blocking item that nobody may resolve is a design that cannot start, so Rev 2 decides it. **D-21: the Config journal is LittleFS files; the raw-write region is void.** The decisive argument is not convenience but arithmetic: `EMMC-FS-01` gives Config `prog_size` 256 B, so an append into an open file costs **one 256-byte program** inside the current block — the block was erased once and is programmed unit by unit until full — and **a raw region cannot offer a smaller program unit, because the unit is a property of the eMMC and not of the filesystem.** What LittleFS adds over a raw region is metadata, single-digit percent at a 32-byte record. The cost the clause was actually aimed at is §3.5's: `np_module_map_persist()` rewriting all 14,012 bytes of the inventory blob to record a 16-byte fact, which at a per-event cadence gives ~4.6 years at clinic rate — and **D-5 already removes that** by giving Map 3 its own file. *The clause was aimed at the right symptom and the wrong cause.* Recorded per NP-CONV-001 §7 as a correction, not a preference: the clause was reasonable when written, predating the hex-tile architecture, when "high-frequency Config updates" meant the session counter and not a per-module journal; what is retired is the justification, while the underlying concern — write granularity is a first-class design parameter — is retained and is why D-24 exists. **Two further grounds against the alternative of shrinking `block_count`.** It **relocates every Config file, including the UKMD record**, without which a user's UHDR partition cannot be mounted at all and of which NeurOne holds no second copy (CLAUDE.md §5.1) — free today because no device is provisioned, but **the window closes at first provisioning, not first ship**, the same boundary error §7.2 corrects for the blob version bump, and worth naming twice because both times the intuitive boundary is later than the real one. And it means re-implementing, by hand at Class B and with no upstream test suite, exactly the three properties §4 depends on — power-loss-safe commit ordering, per-record CRC, wear levelling — which is the wrong answer to OI-NVRAM-12's complaint that those properties rest on an unmanaged dependency. **D-22 closes OI-NVRAM-02:** the UKMD record becomes a named LittleFS file rather than *"Config partition offset 0x1000"*, and `NP-FW-EMMC-002` → Rev 3 §C.3 is corrected in the same change — provably safe because **no code ever used the offset**: `np_uhdr_key.c` has always reached the record through `np_uhdr_hal_config_read_ukmd()`/`_write_ukmd()`, so it was a specification-side artifact only. **D-23 decides OI-NVRAM-03** by widening `EMMC-CFG-01`'s contents list and `EMMC-CFG-02`'s normal-operation whitelist to include the `"NPMP"` blob and the Map 3 journal, rather than forbidding a write that is correct and whose specification predates the architecture — as Rev 1 predicted. **D-24 closes OI-NVRAM-13:** a 32-byte record, chosen so eight fit exactly one 256-byte program unit and none straddles one (requirement 1 of §4.2(b)); a 49,152-byte file — not `file_max` = 65,536 B, deliberately, so a future 40-byte row still fits without re-deciding the bound, which a **record**'s tail-additive-forever policy requires and a cache's would not; 1,536 rows, ~19 per socket at 80 sockets, against the stated assumption of one row per module per session. That is **19 days of margin at home cadence and 1.6 days at clinic cadence** — the clinic figure stated rather than rounded away, and acceptable for three reasons that are not optimism: a clinic device is attached to the control software for most of its duty cycle; exceeding the assumption costs **detail, never a session and never a total**, because §6.3 keeps running totals, drops per-window detail and sets `detail_lost`; and the failure is **visible**, because `detail_lost` is a flag the control software reads rather than a silent truncation. **OI-NVRAM-11 closed** with the sizing comment in `np_module_map.h`, which carried both the stale v2 figures (139 B/socket, 17,804 B) after the v3 calibration payload took `REC_BYTES` to 175 and the blob to 22,412, **and** the `0x1000` UKMD address D-22 retires. `NP-FW-EMMC-001` is a `.docx` (OI-CONV-04) and is not edited; its three clause changes are raised as **ECR-EMMC-001** with replacement text (§3.3.1.4). OI-NVRAM-01 is downgraded from BLOCKING to a documentation action — the design question it blocked is answered and **Map 3 is implementable against Rev 2 now**. OI-NVRAM-05 is untouched and still blocks NP-MOD-ID-001 §10's factory-reset rotation test; it is a factory-reset correctness question, not a storage-layer one. No code behaviour changed.
- **NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 1 — LittleFS is not unmanaged SOUP, it is absent; and the Class B argument turns out to belong to the caller (2026-09-13, Issue #339, supersedes OI-NVRAM-12).** `NP-SW-001` §9.4 had carried LittleFS as SW-02 Class B SOUP with the version *"2.x"* and the verification *"Power-loss testing per LittleFS test suite"* — a cell that names no version and records only that an upstream project tests its own code. **That cell is the defect**: a SOUP verification entry must record what NeurOne did, and this one asserts the existence of diligence rather than performing it, while reading as green in an audit. **Checking it found something stronger than "unmanaged": the component is not in the tree.** `grep -rn "lfs_" firmware/` returns **two hits, both comments** — `np_log_backend.h:79` and `:83`, naming `lfs_file_write` and `lfs_file_sync` as what the OI-LOG-06/07 HAL seams will eventually call. No source under `firmware/vendor/` (which holds `cmsis_core`, `cmsis_device_g0`, `freertos`, `mcux_sdk` — four directories, no fifth), nothing links it, nothing calls it. It is **nominated** SOUP, not integrated SOUP, and every power-loss atomicity guarantee in NP-FW-NVRAM-001 §4, `EMMC-FS-01` and NP-FW-HUB-001 §6.5 rests on it. The absence has been invisible precisely because the layer above the seam is real, tested and passing. **The IEC 62304 §7.1.2 anomaly evaluation is deliberately NOT performed, and saying so is the honest output.** The clause evaluates the published anomaly list *for the version actually in use*; `2.x` is a major-version family spanning years, and recording a conclusion against it would reproduce OI-DOC-01 — a record that resolves to nothing — inside the document written to close its sibling. §7 names the blocking action instead: pin a named upstream release tag, vendor it with per-file provenance and SHA-256 following the `firmware/vendor/cmsis_core/VERSION` pattern (OI-LFS-01), *then* evaluate (OI-LFS-02). OI-LFS-01 also blocks #75 (NP-SBOM-001) for the T2 510(k) cybersecurity submission, because an SBOM entry reading `2.x` is not an SBOM entry. **The hazard analysis IS performed, does not depend on a version, and is the half that matters now** — what a filesystem anomaly can *reach* is a property of the callers, which exist and can be read. Note that the Class B precedent set by the MCUX row is **unavailable** here: that row's argument is reachability (compiled into `np_application` and nothing else), and a device-header layer fails loudly with a blast radius of one SW item, whereas a filesystem carries integrity guarantees for stored values that other code later trusts, so it fails quietly. Three consumer families: the **session log**, which nothing on the device reads back, so an anomaly is records loss and not emission (RISK-LFS-01, Low); **UID-keyed dose calibration**, which **refuses to fall back** exactly as `np_hex_addr_pack()` refuses to mask, so corruption yields *no* calibration and no drive rather than a *default* calibration — the cost is the $11.53/tile driver-plus-metering line, $346/headset at 30 tiles, a differentiator loss and not a hazard; and the **`"NPMP"` blob**, which carries Map 1's per-element **safe ranges** and is therefore the one path from a filesystem anomaly toward drive magnitude. **Conclusion: Class B stands — but on a property of the caller, not of the component.** Three barriers stand between a corrupted range and an emission, and only the first belongs to LittleFS: a CRC over the blob; **reject-and-rebuild**, which makes `np_module_map` discard the blob and re-enumerate from the sockets so that *every integrity failure leaves the inventory empty rather than wrong* (the claim NP-FW-NVRAM-001 §9 turns its Class B argument on); and the 62 °C junction throttle, **a thermal limit standing in for an optical one** (OI-NVRAM-10). And NP-FW-NVRAM-001 §7.2 establishes, for entirely independent and correct reasons, that **Map 3 must have the opposite policy** — tail-additive forever, never discard-and-rebuild, because a cache and a record need opposite disciplines. Both decisions are right; together they make the classification load-bearing on which file is which. Hence **REQ-LFS-01: no value that bounds an emission may be stored under a tail-additive policy.** Safe ranges, calibration and inventory stay in the reject-and-rebuild cache; Map 3's journal carries ordinals, counts and totals and must never become the source of a limit. If that is ever breached, the classification must be re-derived and the thermal cutoff becomes the *only* remaining bound rather than the third of three. OI-LFS-03 asks for the CI check that makes the rule observable rather than remembered, on the `warranty-nojoin-ci.yml` pattern. `NP-SW-001` → Rev 5, with a second Class B SOUP note explaining why this row needed its own record where MCUX did not.
- **2026-09-14 — corrective eyewear is answered with an optic, not with a bigger goggle; `OI-ACC-01` closes, and the accessory criterion gets its first cross-class falsification (`NP-HW-FITOVER-001` Rev 1; `NP-ACC-PRIORITY-001` Rev 2; Issue #344):** `OI-ACC-01` was raised on 2026-09-13 by building the accessory ranking, because **nothing in the document set said whether the goggle assembly could be worn over corrective eyewear** — not `docs/reference/modality-stack.md`, not `NP-TOOL-LENS-001` Rev 2, not `NP-HELMET-GEOM-001`, not `docs/ABBREVIATIONS.md`. **Decided (principal): users who want correction while wearing the goggles buy prescription lenses *for* the goggle assembly — the S3 prescription clip and Rx insert — and there is no intent at this time to build a goggle sized to be worn over the user's own glasses.** The decision is stated positively rather than as an omission: **correction is delivered by putting the right optic *in* the assembly, at the lens plane the entire visual modality is characterised against, not by enlarging the cavity so a second, uncharacterised optic can sit behind it.** It also keeps one lens rim, which is the only condition under which `NP-TOOL-LENS-001` Rev 2's single-tooling-family property (common rail geometry across S1/S2/S3 shade bodies) survives — and, the load-bearing reason, it keeps **one visual safety case**. **The fit-over alternative is specified and deferred rather than unconsidered:** `NP-HW-FITOVER-001` Rev 1 records eight requirements (`REQ-FITOVER-01…08`), an inventory of what an enlarged cavity reopens in currently-locked constraints, and an EXTERNAL **demand gate** — it will not be implemented without specific user requests. The gate's signal instruments already exist (CLAUDE.md §6.3's "would participate" intent flags and pledge mechanism, §2.2's at-cost-upgrade-as-intent-signal pattern, S3 optician-partner contact volume) and **the threshold is deliberately left unset**, because a number invented with no fleet and no S3 programme running would be a number chosen to look decisive. **Two findings make the deferral a design decision rather than a scheduling one, and both are safety rather than sizing.** **(i) A spectacle lens sits inside the interlock path; a shade never does.** `NP-HFE-001` CT-03 argues explicitly that the visual interlocks are *not shade-dependent* — *"shade selection affects comfort/immersion, not the safety interlock"* — and **that argument does not transfer, for a positional reason:** the snap-on shade system mounts **outboard** of the lens, while the 940 nm IR eye-open sensor (`np_mod_visual_hal_ir_eye_open()`, `OI-VIS-03`) looks **inboard**, toward the eye. A shade is never between the sensor and the eye; a user's spectacle lens always is, and it is user-supplied, so its 940 nm behaviour is unknown and varies by material (CR-39, polycarbonate, high-index), by AR coating stack, and by photochromic state. The specific failure is a coating returning 940 nm strongly reading as an eye where there is none — a **false eye-open**, the permissive direction, on one of the three independent layers of a Class C interlock (CLAUDE.md §4.2). This is compounded by **`OI-BENCH-08`**, already open: `np_mod_visual_hal_hall_lifted()` carries three different documented meanings in one file (*"goggles off head"* / *"magnet present → goggles seated"* / *"goggles must be on head"*), which is a clarity defect on the committed assembly but becomes load-bearing once *"seated"* has a second thing it could mean. **(ii) Mode F would meter a dose through an optic it cannot characterise.** Mode F is 808–830 nm retinal PBM during normal-looking wear, and the platform's PBM discipline throughout is *metered* dose. NIR transmission at 808–830 nm varies widely across lens materials and coatings, UV/IR-attenuating coatings are common, and **none of it is disclosed on a prescription** — so a dose through an undisclosed attenuator is a metered *emission* with an unknown delivered fraction. `NP-HW-FITOVER-001` §5.2 recommends **withdrawing Mode F** on such an assembly rather than de-rating it, the same shape of answer as the §13.2c precedent (withhold the claim rather than redefine the parameter to fit the hardware). **The consequence is the strongest argument for the decision: the deferred alternative is not merely later than the committed path, it is capability-*negative* against it** — a fit-over user would lose a modality the S3 user keeps. **Effects on the ranking (`NP-ACC-PRIORITY-001` Rev 2).** Rank 2, the S3 Rx system, had been held at **C1 (access)** on a flagged judgement because `OI-ACC-01` was unanswered; it is now **confirmed and derived** — the platform provides exactly one path by which this cohort reaches the visual modality, which is §3.1's second form of C1 precisely (*a channel the platform delivers but a given user cannot receive*). **No row moved**, which is §3.5's one-class property working as designed. The fit-over assembly itself was then placed by that procedure, applied without reference to the principal's own words: class **C5** (access is already delivered to the cohort by rank 2, so a second route unlocks nothing; dose, protocol, concurrency and envelope all unchanged), below rank 18 on reach, highest engineering cost in the set on Test 4 — **rank 19 of 19, bottom**. The principal had independently called it **"very low priority"**. **That is the cross-class falsification Rev 1 recorded as missing:** D-3 had only ever compared two items inside one class-pair, so C1-over-C2-over-C3 was argued rather than tested, and this case turns precisely on C5 sitting below C1. It is a real test because the criterion could have put it high — the naive reading is that a fit-over assembly serves the *same* access need as rank 2, which would make it C1 near the top, and §3.1's C1 test is what refuses that. **Still not falsified:** the C2–C3 and C3–C4 boundaries have no decided case either way. **Also recorded:** the deferral forecloses nothing (§6.3) — a fit-over goggle is a *separate assembly*, not a feature of the committed one, so no boss, socket or reserved volume is needed to keep it possible, which is the exact opposite of the mastoid-pad anchor boss and is why this document asks for no tooling decision. `NP-HW-FITOVER-001` §8 raises **no open items, deliberately**: §7's four preconditions are recorded as preconditions rather than open items so a deferred concept does not put work on anyone's blocking list, and they become open items on the day the demand gate lifts, not before. The decision is also recorded where a reader would look for it — `docs/reference/modality-stack.md`'s S3 bullet now states that the S3 path is the **only** path for corrective-eyewear users and why. CLAUDE.md untouched; no price set, no locked decision changed, no requirement altered.
- **The study-descriptor signature gets something to check: a canonical signing form, an Ed25519 verifier over it, and the finding that two platforms were about to disagree about what a descriptor says (2026-09-14 — `OI-CONSENT-07` partly addressed, deliberately NOT closed; NP-SW-PORTAL-API-001 Rev 2 §4a; issue #338):** §5.3 has said since it was locked that study descriptors arrive *cryptographically signed*, and `OI-CONSENT-03` built the gate that checks the signature — but **nothing said what is signed**, so the only verifier that could exist was one that refuses. That is not a gap a transport closes. **The one schema decision the spec takes, and why it is the exception.** `NP-SW-PORTAL-API-001` §0 specifies no endpoint and no body on purpose, because writing a URL before the key question is settled makes the unsolved part look solved. The canonical signing form is exempt because a verifier cannot exist without it and two platforms cannot agree without it being written down rather than inferred from whichever serializer each happens to use. It is therefore specified **in code** — `StudyDescriptorCanonicalForm` on both platforms — and pinned by its exact bytes in a test on each, because a form described in prose twice is a form that drifts. **Four properties are decisions, not formatting.** *Length-prefixed rather than delimited*: `studyTitle` is the one field that is server-supplied text and stays text, so it can contain the separator, and a delimiter-joined encoding lets a crafted title shift the field boundaries until two different descriptors produce the same bytes — a signature over an ambiguous encoding signs more than one message, and both suites carry that forgery attempt. *Sets sorted, lists left alone*: `requestedElements` has no inherent order, so the encoding must impose one or the same descriptor signs differently depending on iteration order; `researchCategories` keeps the order the issuer sent. *Stable wire spellings*: iOS names categories and elements by `rawValue`, already its persisted Codable value, and Android now by an explicit `wireName` with `displayName` **derived from it** — so when localization reaches `:core` (§17, `check-locale-strings.ts` PENDING_PATHS) the display side can become a key lookup without moving the wire. All twenty strings are pinned on both sides: changing one invalidates every descriptor already issued, which should be a decision and not a rename. *UTC days rather than instants* — **and this one is a finding.** iOS modelled the issue date as a `Date` and Android as `"yyyy-MM-dd"`, so the coarser representation is the only one both can produce; anything finer would have had one platform computing different bytes for the same descriptor and rejecting every signature the other accepted, discovered at integration rather than here. The consequence is stated rather than left to be found: **time of day is not covered by the signature**, so an issuer must not rely on it to distinguish two descriptors — `studyID` does that. The day renders in UTC on a fixed Gregorian calendar, so a descriptor cannot verify in one time zone and fail in another. **The audit-trail hash digests the signed bytes**, not the parsed object, or §5.3's trail would record a hash of the device's reading of a descriptor rather than of the descriptor. **Ed25519, and one key format.** Deterministic signatures (no per-signature entropy for the signing side to get wrong), small keys, and on the JDK since 15 — which matters because Android's `:core` is pure-JVM by design and may not reach for an Android provider. The key is the **raw 32 bytes on both platforms** even though the JDK's `KeyFactory` wants SubjectPublicKeyInfo: if each side took what its own library prefers, "the NeurOne study-signing key" would be two artifacts and a build could ship the wrong one to one platform. The 12-byte DER prefix is fixed for the algorithm, so that conversion belongs in the verifier rather than in whoever holds the key. **Why this does not close the item.** `Ed25519StudyDescriptorVerifier` cannot be constructed without a public key and there is no key to construct it with — §4(a) requires it to ship *with the app build*, pinned, because a key fetched over the channel it authenticates proves only that one party sent both, which TLS already proves. `RefusingStudyDescriptorVerifier` stays the `ConsentStore` default, and an absent, malformed or wrong-length key yields **no verifier at all** rather than one that throws at the first descriptor, so a misconfigured build falls back to refusing rather than to a crash on the first study. A malformed *signature*, by contrast, is `rejected` and never `unavailable`: `unavailable` means the device cannot check, and a device holding a key can — collapsing the two would let a forged descriptor read as a configuration problem. What remains is the key: who holds it, how it is rotated, how it reaches a build. **10 new unit tests per platform**; Android `:core` 240 → 250 green here, iOS's parallel file is CI's first compile. **No locked decision changes.**
- **2026-09-14 — One Xcode project convention for both Apple targets: the XcodeGen spec is committed, the generated `.xcodeproj` is not (GitHub Issue #189).** The repository carried two conventions at once: `app/ios/NeurOne.xcodeproj` was tracked and `ios-ci.yml` built it directly, while `app/watchos/NeurOneWatch.xcodeproj` was git-ignored and `watchos-ci.yml` regenerated it from `app/watchos/project.yml` (PR #190). **Resolved in the watchOS direction (the issue's Option A), on the argument CLAUDE.md §17.5 already makes about locale files** — *a generated file under version control is a second source of truth whether or not anyone means it to be*, and detection is not enough, because it cannot stop the edit being made in the wrong file when the wrong file is sitting in the working tree, tracked and editable. **The drift was not hypothetical, and finding it is most of what this entry records.** PR #285 added the bundled `protocols/predefined` folder reference to the committed `.pbxproj` by hand and never to `project.yml`; PR #107 had done the same for `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` (ISC-133). Regenerating the project at any point in the last three weeks would therefore have shipped an app with **no protocol library at all** — `NPBundledProtocols` reads `predefined/manifest.json` out of the bundle, and `NPProtocolLibraryTests` asserts on it. The folder is now a source entry in the spec; `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` needs none, because XcodeGen's default build phase for an unrecognised extension is Resources and the `NeurOne` source scan reaches it. Every other file in the committed project was verified to be reproducible from the spec: the file set referenced by the `.pbxproj` equals the on-disk source tree exactly, and every build setting in it traces to `project.yml` or to XcodeGen's setting presets. **The ordering rule this exposed is the opposite of the one the issue proposed.** The issue's implementation note said `xcodegen generate` must land *before* the existing `bun scripts/sync-locales.ts` step. It must land **after** it. XcodeGen builds the project from what is on disk when it runs, so generating against a fresh checkout — where `Localizable.xcstrings` is git-ignored and absent — produces a project with **no Resources entry for the catalogue** (the Rev 41 raw-key failure reached by a second route: every `String(localized:)` renders its key) and with **`knownRegions` collapsed from the eleven locales to `en` + `Base`**, because XcodeGen reads a project's known regions out of the String Catalog itself. Neither absence fails a build, which is what makes it the §17-shaped hazard rather than an inconvenience. `optional: true` was considered and does not help: XcodeGen creates no file reference for an optional path that is missing, it merely suppresses the error. The order is therefore stated three times — the step order in `ios-ci.yml`, a note at the generator's iOS output path in `sync-locales.ts`, and `options.preGenCommand` in `app/ios/project.yml`, which runs the generator from the repository root before XcodeGen reads anything and aborts generation with a named remedy if `bun` is not on `PATH`. The last one is what makes `cd app/ios && xcodegen generate` safe as a bootstrap command for a developer who has never heard of any of this. **Also removed:** a `preBuildScripts` block under `schemes.NeurOne` in the spec, a non-blocking SwiftLint phase that had never reached any project — `preBuildScripts` is a target key and XcodeGen ignores it under a scheme, and the committed `.pbxproj` had no such phase either. It is deleted rather than carried into the file that is now the only source of truth, with a comment saying where it would go if wanted; linting remains the `swiftlint --strict` job in `ios-ci.yml`. **Not verified, and stated rather than implied:** XcodeGen runs only on macOS, so nothing in this change was generated or built before it was pushed. The first `ios-ci` run is the check. The claims about XcodeGen's behaviour above were read off the generator's source (`SourceGenerator.swift`, `PBXProjGenerator.swift`, `GenerateCommand.swift`) rather than assumed. **Left open, closed 2026-09-22:** `ios-ci.yml`'s `paths:` filter still does not include `protocols/**`, so an edit to a bundled `.npps` — now demonstrably a build input to the iOS app — does not run the workflow whose tests load it. *Closed:* both `ios-ci.yml` triggers now list `protocols/predefined/**` — the exact folder the spec bundles, not all of `protocols/`. Android bundles no protocol library, so `android-ci.yml` is unchanged.
- **2026-09-14 — five FAI checklist IDs were cited across the document set as controlled documents; four of them were duplicate serials, not missing documents (`OI-ART-05` CLOSED; GitHub Issue #343):** `NP-FAI-CVNS-001`, `NP-FAI-HD-001`, `NP-FAI-SM-001`, `NP-FAI-CV-001` and `NP-FAI-ANON-001` appeared in `NP-DHF-001` §8, in `NP-ART-001`, in `NP-PRIV-REM-001` STEP-24, in `NP-RISK-002` RISK-25, in a `firmware/CMakeLists.txt` registration and — worst of the set — as the *document header printed by two test binaries*. None had ever been written. The design-control risk is specific and is what made this worth doing: a serial with no document behind it, cited in a test name and printed by a passing suite, reads to a reviewer and to the DHF index exactly like a controlled document that has been executed. **Principal finding: the document set was not missing five checklists. It was carrying four second names.** Every item group the four serials were supposed to hold is already specified, in full, inside a firmware specification's own FAI section — FAI-SM-01…11 in `NP-FW-PBM1064-001` §11, FAI-HD01…HD04 in `NP-FW-HD-001` §12, FAI-CV01…CV03 in `NP-FW-CVNS-001` §9, FAI-ANON-01…09 in `NP-FW-ANON-001` §9 (three BASELINED, one ACTIVE). So the four are **retired**, those sections are the record of file, and every live citation is re-pointed at them. `NP-FAI-CV-001` was doubly redundant — it and `NP-FAI-CVNS-001` were two serials for the same artifact (A14). **The fifth is a real absence and is deliberately NOT closed.** `NP-FAI-CVNS-001` fails `NP-FAI-001` §2 F1 outright: no mechanical or electrical specification exists for the A14 cervical VNS electrode assembly, cable or connector (Issue #332), so there is nothing to inspect against. §2 requires exactly this case to be recorded as a *named absence* in `NP-ART-001` §3.2 rather than written up with `TBD` accept criteria, and that is where it stays (`OI-FAI-07`). **Each of the other four was also tested as an artifact checklist in its own right, and none passes either:** the smart-module hardware items (FAI-SM-04, -06, -07, -08) inspect artifact A2, whose checklist is already named `NP-FAI-HEXFPC-001` and blocked on `OI-HEXTILE-02/-12/-05` (`OI-FAI-05`); the HD-tDCS bench limbs inspect the T2 clinical electrode cap, whose only specification is DRAFT and which has **no artifact-register row at all** (`OI-FAI-06`, and new `OI-ART-08`); and research anonymisation is outside the FAI programme's scope entirely (`NP-FAI-001` §1 excludes software verification, `NP-ART-001` §1 excludes firmware modules), so it never needed a serial — `NP-PRIV-REM-001` STEP-22 and STEP-23 already name verification tests without one, and STEP-24 was the outlier. **No checklist was written, because writing one would have been the failure `NP-FAI-001` §2 exists to prevent.** **Root cause, and it is closed rather than merely noted:** `FAI-<GROUP><NN>` numbers an *item*, and `NP-CONV-001` §6's identifier-family table never listed the FAI family, so nothing said where an `FAI-` group lives — which made "there must be an `NP-FAI-<GROUP>-001`" a reasonable inference. `NP-FAI-001` **§3.1** now states that an item prefix never implies a document serial, that the owning document is named explicitly or the item is not cited (a bare `FAI-HD03` in a banner or risk row is not a citation), and that artifact checklist serials come only from `NP-ART-001` §3. **Deliberately left as findable absences rather than swept:** the ID still appears in `NP-ART-001` §2.4/§3.2 (the rows whose job is to record that A14's checklist does not exist), in `NP-CONV-001` §4.0's worked example of *"a document can be named before it exists"*, and in revision histories and this log — `NP-CONV-001` §1.1 protects records of what was written, and deleting them would destroy the evidence that made the gap findable in the first place. `NP-FAI-001` §2.1.2 states those two exceptions explicitly so a later sweep does not undo them. Everywhere else — the DHF index, firmware banners, CMake registrations, remediation-step document fields, live specification prose — a retired serial now appears nowhere, and **no firmware file mentions any of the five**. **Also corrected, both pre-existing:** `NP-DHF-001` §7's design-verification row said *eleven of the twelve* artifact FAI checklists cannot be written — that counted `NP-ART-001` §3.2 table rows, one of which covers four artifacts, so it is **fourteen of the fifteen**; and `NP-RISK-002` RISK-25 attributed its blocked FAI bench to the checklist's non-existence, inverting cause and effect. **Nothing in the engineering content moved:** no FAI item number renumbered, no accept criterion created, changed or deleted, no risk re-scored, no constant, limit or firmware behaviour touched — the two affected test binaries print a different document header and are otherwise identical in behaviour (ctest unchanged). Revised: `NP-FAI-001` Rev 2 (§2.1, §3.1, OI-FAI-05/-06/-07), `NP-ART-001` Rev 2 (OI-ART-05 closed, OI-ART-08 raised), `NP-DHF-001` Rev 49, `NP-FW-HD-001` Rev 6, `NP-FW-CVNS-001` Rev 4, `NP-PRIV-REM-001` Rev 5, `NP-RISK-002` Rev 2. Per development-mode posture (no hardware, no bench data, no executed inspection) **no QMS/CAPA record was opened** — this is document control on a documented absence, not a defect filing.
- **littlefs pinned at v2.11.3 and vendored — OI-LFS-01 CLOSED, and the blocking status moved rather than lifted (2026-09-14, NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 2, NP-SW-001 Rev 6, DHF Rev 50).** NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 1 found that LittleFS was not merely unmanaged SOUP but **absent from the tree**, and left `OI-LFS-01` blocking every power-loss atomicity claim in the document set plus #75 (NP-SBOM-001). That item is now performed. **What was done:** five byte-exact files — `lfs.c`, `lfs.h`, `lfs_util.c`, `lfs_util.h`, `LICENSE.md` — vendored at `firmware/vendor/littlefs/` from tag `v2.11.3` (commit `6cb4e865`, BSD-3-Clause), byte-exactness established by two independent clones and a per-file comparison, with per-file SHA-256 in a `VERSION` record on the `firmware/vendor/cmsis_core/VERSION` pattern. **The tag choice is a safety argument, not recency, and that is the part worth keeping:** `v2.11.3` is the first release containing upstream `488e84bb`, *"Fixed data corruption with multiple write handles"* — a sync through one handle left a second open handle on the same file stale, after which the allocator could re-use still-referenced blocks. That is a data-corruption defect on exactly the axis claims `L-1…L-4` rest on, so any older tag would have been knowingly pinning a version carrying it. It also leaves a NeurOne requirement behind: no file on any instance may be open through two handles, which is true of the three Config files and two log files *by accident of how the design reads* rather than by anything that would notice if it stopped — `OI-LFS-06`. **Upstream's own `tests/` are deliberately NOT vendored**, and the reason is specific: the defective SOUP cell this whole record replaced cited that suite as NeurOne's verification, so vendoring it would make the citation look discharged without anything having been run. **The configuration is part of the record** (NP-SOUP-LFS-001 §7.3) and is first-party code in `hub_control` on the FreeRTOSConfig.h precedent — `np_lfs_config.h`, `np_lfs_instance.{h,c}` — so the vendored directory stays a byte-exact copy: `LFS_NO_MALLOC` with static buffers (the device heap is a fixed 64 KiB FreeRTOS pool, and `lfs_file_open()` is unusable by construction — only `lfs_file_opencfg()`); `LFS_THREADSAFE` **on**, because the Config instance already has three specified writers whose callers are not one task and the alternative is a single-task assumption recorded nowhere; assertions **retargeted rather than removed**, to a handler that halts the main processor so the safety MCU's 1.5 s watchdog cuts all stimulation (the `np_freertos_assert_failed` discipline), because `lfs_init()`'s configuration checks *are* assertions; `block_cycles` **500** set explicitly and checked *by value*, because `-1` passes every "is it non-zero" test and silently disables the wear levelling claim `L-6` rests on. **Why a NeurOne validator exists at all when littlefs validates its own config:** `lfs_init()`'s assertions check that a configuration is *internally consistent* and cannot check that it is *NeurOne's* — a config with `block_size` 512 and `block_count` 32,768 passes every one of them and mounts a geometry no document describes. `np_lfs_config_validate()` is that missing half and is the mount-time check claim `L-5` names. **One finding came out of building it**, and it is the kind that is invisible until something compiles: setting `LFS_NO_ASSERT` beside a replacement `LFS_ASSERT` — reading the define as "do not include `<assert.h>`" — removes the **definition** of `lfs_mlist_isopen` (`lfs.c:507`) while `lfs.c:6178` keeps the **call** inside `LFS_ASSERT`, giving an undefined symbol that appears only once something pulls `lfs.o` into a link. Patching vendored SOUP is not available (NP-SW-CI-001 §9), so the configuration yielded — and what that assertion checks is that a file is not already open through a second handle, i.e. the run-time detector for the very corruption the tag was pinned for. **`np_lfs_config_tests`** (Class B host target 27 → 28, repo total 34 → 35) recomputes every vendored file's SHA-256 against the `VERSION` record with `firmware/crypto`'s SHA-256, compares the build configuration against what that record tells a reviewer to rely on, pins the EMMC-FS-01 instance parameters, and requires **19 single-field perturbations of a known-good config to be rejected**; per NP-CONV-001 §8 it was falsified in seven ways first (corrupted recorded hash, edited vendored byte, `block_cycles` `-1` and `100`, `lookahead_size` 16, a hole punched in the validator, `LFS_THREADSAFE` removed), with the baseline confirmed passing again after each. **THE PART THAT MATTERS MOST IS WHAT DID NOT CHANGE.** Nothing calls the library — `OI-LOG-05..07` are unimplemented — IEC 62304 §7.1.2 is still undischarged, and no power-loss behaviour has been exercised by anything. `NP-FW-NVRAM-001` §4, `EMMC-FS-01` and `NP-FW-HUB-001` §6.5 are **exactly as unverified as they were the day before**; `OI-LFS-02` (the §7.1.2 evaluation plus a NeurOne power-loss injection test against `L-1…L-4`, falsified in both directions) **inherits `OI-LFS-01`'s BLOCKING status**, and both of those documents were amended so neither reads as unblocked. NP-SOUP-LFS-001 §7.5 exists specifically because a populated `firmware/vendor/` directory reads as an answer. **Rev 1's stated reason for not pinning is outweighed, not refuted** (NP-CONV-001 §7): it remains true that a pinned tag with an unread anomaly list is not a discharged §7.1.2, but "do not pin before the list is read" blocked its own precondition — the list cannot be read until a tag exists — and blocked #75 besides. Recording the evaluation's *absence* explicitly is a record that resolves to something, which is what OI-DOC-01 asks for. **Two open items raised while doing it.** `OI-LFS-05`: `EMMC-FS-01` states instance parameters for the **Config** partition only, and `L-1`/`L-2` — the two claims NP-FW-HUB-001 §6.5's durability model rests on — are claims about the **log** partitions, whose `block_count` at 512 MiB and 6,903 MiB is 131,072 and 1,767,168 against Config's 4,096, so a full-coverage `lookahead_size` would be 16 KiB and 216 KiB rather than Config's 512 B; `np_lfs_instance.h` is deliberately scoped so it cannot be reused for them by analogy. `OI-LFS-06`: make "one open handle per file" checkable rather than remembered. `RISK-LFS-04` (an unpinned version reaching the SBOM) is **closed** — `v2.11.3` + commit + per-file SHA-256 is an SBOM entry; `RISK-LFS-03` is narrowed and explicitly *not* removed; `RISK-LFS-05` added for the log instances. **REQ-LFS-01 and the §6.2 conclusion are untouched** — the Class B classification is still a property of `np_module_map`'s reject-and-rebuild policy rather than of littlefs, and pinning a version does not and could not change that.
- **The littlefs §7.1.2 anomaly evaluation is performed and `L-1…L-4` are exercised — OI-LFS-02 CLOSED, and the evaluation's real output is two rules for code that does not exist yet (2026-09-14, NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 2 → 3, NP-SW-001 Rev 6 → 7 §9.4, NP-DHF-001 Rev 51).** **Follows the entry above it**, which pinned the version and said plainly that pinning verified nothing. Two activities. **(1) The IEC 62304 §7.1.2 evaluation (NP-SOUP-LFS-001 §11).** littlefs publishes no numbered errata document, so the anomaly list is its GitHub tracker plus the release notes — five queries against it, the `v2.11.2`→`v2.11.3` differential, and thirteen items assessed against the seven claims in §3. **Recorded because it is the kind of thing a later reviewer repeats: there is no `bug` label, and a query for one returns nothing — an empty result there is not a clean one.** Three items are applicable. #1086's assertion during metadata relocation becomes the retargeted halt, which stops the SPI heartbeat and makes the safety MCU cut all stimulation — the second time the decision to keep assertions ON has paid. #1205 leaves stale bytes in the read cache after a block-device read error and returns them **reporting success**, and what stops it is not the component but the callers: every stored value is CRC- or AEAD-checked at the point of use, so a wrong value becomes an absence. **#1210 is stopped by nothing that exists today** — compaction can drop a file's `NAME` tag while keeping its `INLINE` data, silently, with a valid CRC, so no integrity check fires and the caller never learns the file is gone; the case to design against is `ukmd.rec`, whose loss makes that user's UHDR **permanently unmountable** with no NeurOne-held second copy. **That is NP-SOUP-LFS-001 §6.2's finding arriving a second time by a different route**: the anomaly profile, like the Class B argument, rests on caller policy, and the callers are the unwritten `OI-LOG-05..07` glue. So the evaluation's output is two new caller rules — `OI-LFS-08` (bound the Config directory's create/delete churn; stop `ukmd.rec` resting on one filesystem entry) and `OI-LFS-09` (validate by content on **every** read: `lfs_stat()` is not a durability check, because upstream #1164 establishes that a file can survive a power loss with none of its data) — stated at the cheapest moment, before the code exists. **(2) The power-loss injection test (§12).** `np_lfs_powerloss_tests`, Class B host target 29, repo 35 → 36. NeurOne's own injecting block device (`np_lfs_powerbd.c`); upstream's `bd/lfs_emubd.*` stays unvendored **precisely because the defective SOUP cell this whole record replaced cited upstream's suite as NeurOne's verification**. A power loss is modelled as a stop, not an error return: the injector applies the partial physical effect and `longjmp`s out of littlefs, the media array surviving and every byte of RAM state not. A cut is swept across **every** medium-touching op of three commit sequences — a flushed session-log append, the 14,012-byte `"NPMP"` blob replaced write-temp-then-rename, and a tail-additive Map 3 journal — under three tear models, over the real `EMMC-FS-01` geometry, with `np_lfs_config_validate()` required to accept the configuration first. **456 interrupted runs, 0 violations, every attempt remounting cleanly.** `L-1` is checked in a sharper form than it is written: the recovered length must be an **exact flush boundary**, because "a flush commits the exact buffered tail" forbids a partial batch becoming visible. **`L-3`'s result is a property of the ordering, not of the component** — littlefs does not make a replacement atomic, write-temp-then-rename does, and the glue has to use it. Falsified in both directions per NP-CONV-001 §8: three unsafe orderings the same verifiers are **required** to catch on every CI run (including truncate-and-rebuild, which is `REQ-LFS-01`'s own subject matter), plus six hand perturbations — **five caught, one not**: clearing the `lfs_t` across a simulated reboot changes nothing because `lfs_mount()` re-initialises it, and that negative result is recorded rather than dropped, because a falsification record listing only the perturbations that were caught is a record of what somebody chose to try. **What is still not true, and the records say so where the old warnings stood.** The test interrupts the `struct lfs_config` **contract** at `prog_size` granularity; NeurOne's medium is an eMMC behind an XTS layer that may tear inside a 512 B sector and whose erase semantics are not raw-flash semantics — upstream #1083 asks exactly this and has no answer. `OI-LFS-07` is the part of `OI-LFS-02` a host test could never have discharged, and it is raised rather than absorbed. Nothing calls the library; `OI-LOG-05..07` are unimplemented. The sweeps mount the **Config** instance, while `L-1`/`L-2` are claims about the log partitions, whose parameters no document states (`OI-LFS-05`). `L-6` is configured and never measured. A post-power-loss hang is invisible to a sweep. **NP-FW-NVRAM-001 §4, EMMC-FS-01 and NP-FW-HUB-001 §6.5 may now be relied on exactly as far as NP-SOUP-LFS-001 §12.5 states and no further** — and each of those documents was annotated to that depth rather than marked clear.
- **The littlefs caller rules are built — OI-LFS-03, -05, -06, -08, -09 CLOSED (2026-09-24, NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 4 §13, GitHub #382).** §11 of the SOUP record found that what stops littlefs v2.11.3's applicable anomalies is caller policy, and the callers did not exist. New Class B module `np_cfg_store` is the Config instance's only caller and makes each rule a property of its API: a registry refusing a second handle on an open file (OI-LFS-06, upstream 488e84bb); a mandatory content check on every read, no `stat`, and a read error reported as an absence and followed by a remount (OI-LFS-09, upstream #1205/#1164); no remove or rename — replacement is an in-place `O_TRUNC` rewrite, re-swept for L-3 — and `ukmd.rec` as two enveloped copies in two directories, repaired from its twin on first use (OI-LFS-08, upstream #1210); and a per-file policy table. `scripts/check-lfs-caller-rules.ts` (tooling-ci, 21-case self-test) is REQ-LFS-01's gate (OI-LFS-03) and makes one-handle-per-file checkable. New module `np_lfs_log_instance` gives UHDR and SHDR their parameters and a validator (OI-LFS-05). Host tests `np_cfg_store_tests` and `np_lfs_log_instance_tests` (Class B 32 → 34, total 42 → 44). **Decisions and why:** (1) the log instances take **EMMC-FS-01's own UHDR/SHDR columns** — OI-LFS-05 was raised on the belief that none existed, and they do; (2) **one deviation, read/prog 512** (ECR-EMMC-002): EMMC-UHDR-05 sets the XTS unit to 512 "matching" read/prog, EMMC-FS-01 prints 256, and a program smaller than the unit tears committed bytes under a read-modify-write — shown by sweep; (3) **`lookahead_size` kept at the specified 512/256** under CLAUDE.md §18 — full coverage would cost 237 KiB of on-chip RAM (no external SDRAM, OI-SWCI-46) against a stall whose duration no host can measure; the read count is measured (≈ 0.5 reads per block in use per traversal, one traversal per 16 MiB) and the time is added to OI-LFS-07; (4) **in-place O_TRUNC instead of write-temp-then-rename** for the Config store, because rename deletes and delete churn is #1210's trigger. **Findings raised:** OI-LFS-10 (the Config instance disagrees with EMMC-FS-01 on five fields, and its prog_size 256 loses a durable Map 3 record under the RMW model) and OI-LFS-11 (one log file per partition cannot pass the 2 GiB file_max). **Negative results kept:** #1210 did not reproduce in 3,000 churn cycles; the remount was at first caught only by a counter, and following that up found a second #1205 path through the metadata pair. **Still open:** OI-LFS-07 (hardware) and OI-LFS-04 (Safety + EE). Nothing is integrated — OI-LOG-05..07.
- **OI-LFS-10 (hazard half) — the Config littlefs instance programs 512 bytes, not 256 (2026-09-24, principal; NP-SOUP-LFS-001 Rev 5 §13.8, DHF Rev 84).** Supersedes the Config `read_size`/`prog_size` 256 that `np_lfs_instance.h` took from EMMC-FS-01. Why: the partition is XTS-encrypted in 512-byte data units (EMMC-UHDR-05), so a 256-byte program forces a read-modify-write of a unit whose other half littlefs has already committed, and the Rev 4 sweep lost a durable Map 3 record that way. `cache_size` moves to 512 as a consequence (`lfs_init()` requires a multiple of `prog_size`), matching EMMC-FS-01.
